class="nobold">(2021)</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><img alt="Increase" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/11px-Increase2.svg.png" decoding="async" href="#cite_note-UNHDR-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><br /><span class="nowrap"><span style="color:orange">medium</span></span>&#160;·&#160;<a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index" <p><b>Ghana</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="/ɑː/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;father&#39;">ɑː</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span></span>/</a></span>&#32;<span class="nowrap" style="font-size:85%">(<span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:En-us-Ghana.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/17px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/22px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="20" data-file-height="20" /></a></span><a href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e4/En-us-Ghana.ogg" class="internal" title="En-us-Ghana.ogg">listen</a></span></span>)</span></span>; <a href="/wiki/Twi_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Twi language">Twi</a>: <i lang="tw">Gaana</i>, <a href="/wiki/Ewe_language" title="Ewe language">Ewe</a>: <i lang="ee">Gana</i>), officially the <b>Republic of Ghana</b>, is a country in <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West Africa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> It abuts the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Guinea" title="Gulf of Guinea">Gulf of Guinea</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Ocean" title="Atlantic Ocean">Atlantic Ocean</a> to the south, sharing borders with <a href="/wiki/Ivory_Coast" title="Ivory Coast">Ivory Coast</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ghana%E2%80%93Ivory_Coast_border" title="Ghana–Ivory Coast border">the west</a>, <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a> in <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso%E2%80%93Ghana_border" title="Burkina Faso–Ghana border">the north</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Togo" title="Togo">Togo</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ghana%E2%80%93Togo_border" title="Ghana–Togo border">the east</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-warriorking_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-warriorking-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Ghana covers an area of 238,535&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (92,099&#160;sq&#160;mi), spanning diverse biomes that range from coastal savannas to tropical rainforests. With nearly 31 million inhabitants (according to the 2021<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ghana&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> census), Ghana is the <a href="/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_population" title="List of African countries by population">second-most populous country</a> in West Africa, after <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The capital and <a href="/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Ghana" title="List of cities in Ghana">largest city</a> is <a href="/wiki/Accra" title="Accra">Accra</a>; other major cities are <a href="/wiki/Kumasi" title="Kumasi">Kumasi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tamale,_Ghana" title="Tamale, Ghana">Tamale</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Sekondi-Takoradi" title="Sekondi-Takoradi">Sekondi-Takoradi</a>.
</p><p>The first permanent state in present-day Ghana was the <a href="/wiki/Bono_state" title="Bono state">Bono state</a> of the 11th century.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Numerous kingdoms and empires emerged over the centuries, of which the most powerful were the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dagbon" title="Kingdom of Dagbon">Kingdom of Dagbon</a> in the north<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Ashanti_Empire" title="Ashanti Empire">Ashanti Empire</a> in the south.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Beginning in the 15th century, the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese Empire</a>, followed by numerous other European powers, contested the area for trading rights, until the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British</a> ultimately established control of the coast by the late 19th century. Following over a century of colonisation, Ghana's current borders took shape, encompassing four separate British colonial territories: <a href="/wiki/Gold_Coast_(British_colony)" title="Gold Coast (British colony)">Gold Coast</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ashanti_(Crown_Colony)" title="Ashanti (Crown Colony)">Ashanti</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Territories_of_the_Gold_Coast" title="Northern Territories of the Gold Coast">Northern Territories</a>, and <a href="/wiki/British_Togoland" title="British Togoland">British Togoland</a>. These were unified as an independent dominion within the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a> on 6 March 1957, becoming the first colony in West Africa to achieve sovereignty.<sup id="cite_ref-Universal_Newsreel_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Universal_Newsreel-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-First_For_Sub-Saharan_Africa_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-First_For_Sub-Saharan_Africa-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Exploring_Africa_–_Decolinization_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Exploring_Africa_–_Decolinization-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Ghana subsequently became influential in <a href="/wiki/Decolonisation_of_Africa" title="Decolonisation of Africa">decolonisation efforts</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Pan-Africanism" title="Pan-Africanism">Pan-African movement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Ateku_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ateku-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ghana is a multi-ethnic country with a diverse population, linguistic and religious groups;<sup id="cite_ref-popest_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-popest-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> while the <a href="/wiki/Akan_people" title="Akan people">Akan</a> are the largest ethnic group, they constitute only a plurality. Most <a href="/wiki/Ghanaian_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Ghanaian people">Ghanaians</a> are <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_Ghana" title="Christianity in Ghana">Christians</a> (71.3%); almost a fifth are <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Ghana" title="Islam in Ghana">Muslims</a>; a tenth practise traditional faiths or report no religion.<sup id="cite_ref-statsghana1_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-statsghana1-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Ghana is a <a href="/wiki/Unitary_state" title="Unitary state">unitary</a> <a href="/wiki/Liberal_democracy" title="Liberal democracy">constitutional democracy</a> led by a <a href="/wiki/President_of_Ghana" title="President of Ghana">president</a> who is both <a href="/wiki/Head_of_state" title="Head of state">head of state</a> and <a href="/wiki/Head_of_government" title="Head of government">head of government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Ghana:_CIA_World_FactBook_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ghana:_CIA_World_FactBook-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Ghana has maintained since 1993 one of the freest and most stable governments on the continent, and it performs relatively well in <a href="/wiki/Healthcare_in_Ghana" title="Healthcare in Ghana">healthcare</a>, <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Ghana" title="Economy of Ghana">economic</a> growth, and human development,<sup id="cite_ref-Ateku_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ateku-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> so that it has a significant influence in West Africa<sup id="cite_ref-South_America_and_West_Africa_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-South_America_and_West_Africa-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> and is highly integrated in international affairs, being a member of the <a href="/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement" title="Non-Aligned Movement">Non-Aligned Movement</a>, <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>, <a href="/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States" title="Economic Community of West African States">Economic Community of West African States</a>, <a href="/wiki/Group_of_24" title="Group of 24">Group of 24</a>, and Commonwealth of Nations.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ghana&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Etymology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><i>Ghana</i> means "strong warrior king" and was the title accorded to the kings of the medieval <a href="/wiki/Ghana_Empire" title="Ghana Empire">Ghana Empire</a> in <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West Africa</a>—not to be confused with today's Ghana, for the empire was further north, in modern-day <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a>, <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a> and southern <a href="/wiki/Mauritania" title="Mauritania">Mauritania</a>, as well as in <a href="/wiki/Guinea_(region)" title="Guinea (region)">the region of Guinea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-etymology_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-etymology-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Ghana was known for its large gold usage and hence was named the <i>Land of Gold.</i><sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ghana&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Ghana" title="History of Ghana">History of Ghana</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Akan_face.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Akan_face.jpg/170px-Akan_face.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="256" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Akan_face.jpg/255px-Akan_face.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Akan_face.jpg/340px-Akan_face.jpg 2x" data-file-width="681" data-file-height="1024" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Akan_face.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>16th-century <a href="/wiki/Akan_people" title="Akan people">Akan</a> Terracotta, <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_Museum_of_Art" title="Metropolitan Museum of Art">Metropolitan Museum of Art</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Guinea_from_Milner%27s_Atlas.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Guinea_from_Milner%27s_Atlas.jpg/220px-Guinea_from_Milner%27s_Atlas.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="156" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Guinea_from_Milner%27s_Atlas.jpg/330px-Guinea_from_Milner%27s_Atlas.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Guinea_from_Milner%27s_Atlas.jpg/440px-Guinea_from_Milner%27s_Atlas.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1008" data-file-height="717" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Guinea_from_Milner%27s_Atlas.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>An 1850 map showing the <a href="/wiki/Akan_people" title="Akan people">Akan</a> <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ashanti" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of Ashanti">Kingdom of Ashanti</a> within the <a href="/wiki/Guinea_(region)" title="Guinea (region)">Guinea region</a> and surrounding regions in West Africa</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Lidded_Vessel_(Kuduo)_MET_DP108293.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Lidded_Vessel_%28Kuduo%29_MET_DP108293.jpg/220px-Lidded_Vessel_%28Kuduo%29_MET_DP108293.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="247" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Lidded_Vessel_%28Kuduo%29_MET_DP108293.jpg/330px-Lidded_Vessel_%28Kuduo%29_MET_DP108293.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Lidded_Vessel_%28Kuduo%29_MET_DP108293.jpg/440px-Lidded_Vessel_%28Kuduo%29_MET_DP108293.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1744" data-file-height="1960" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Lidded_Vessel_(Kuduo)_MET_DP108293.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>18th-century Ashanti brass <i>kuduo</i>. Gold dust and nuggets were kept in <i>kuduo</i>, as were other items of personal value and significance. As receptacles for their owners' <i>kra</i>, or life force, <i>kuduo</i> were prominent features of ceremonies designed to honour and protect that individual.</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Medieval_kingdoms">Medieval kingdoms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ghana&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Medieval kingdoms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Ashanti_Empire" title="Ashanti Empire">Ashanti Empire</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dagbon" title="Kingdom of Dagbon">Kingdom of Dagbon</a></div>
<p>Most of what is now Ghana was inhabited in the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Age_of_Discovery" title="Age of Discovery">Age of Discovery</a> by different ethnic groups. The earliest known kingdoms to emerge in modern Ghana were the <a href="/wiki/Oti%E2%80%93Volta_languages" title="Oti–Volta languages">Mole-Dagbani</a> states.<sup id="cite_ref-col_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-col-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The Mole-Dagomba came on horseback from present-day <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a> under a single leader, <a href="/wiki/Naa_Gbewaa" title="Naa Gbewaa">Naa Gbewaa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> With their advanced weapons and based on a central authority, they easily invaded and occupied the lands of the local people ruled by the <i>tendamba</i> (land god priests), established themselves as the rulers over the locals, and made <a href="/wiki/Gambaga" title="Gambaga">Gambaga</a> their capital.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The death of Naa Gbewaa caused seccession among his children, some of whom broke off and founded separate states including <a href="/wiki/Mamprusi_people" title="Mamprusi people">Mamprugu</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nanumba_people" title="Nanumba people">Nanung</a>. <sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Whiles Gbewaa was still alive, his daughter <a href="/wiki/Yennenga" title="Yennenga">Yennenga</a>, travelled north and founded the <a href="/wiki/Mossi_Kingdoms" title="Mossi Kingdoms">Mossi Kingdoms</a>, who constitute the majority of present day <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a>. Other kingdoms that emerged from <a href="/wiki/Dagbon" class="mw-redirect" title="Dagbon">Dagbon</a> include the <a href="/wiki/Bouna,_Ivory_Coast" title="Bouna, Ivory Coast">Bouna Kingdom</a> of Ivory Coast, and the <a href="/wiki/Dagaaba_people" title="Dagaaba people">Dagaaba states</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Upper_West_Region" title="Upper West Region">Upper West Region</a>.
</p><p>Although the area of present-day Ghana has experienced many population movements, the <a href="/wiki/Akan_people" title="Akan people">Akan</a>-speaking peoples began to move into it toward the end of the 15th century.<sup id="cite_ref-col_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-col-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> By the early 16th century, the Akans were firmly established in the Akan state called <a href="/wiki/Bono_state" title="Bono state">Bonoman</a>, for which the <a href="/wiki/Brong-Ahafo_region" title="Brong-Ahafo region">Brong-Ahafo region</a> was named.<sup id="cite_ref-col_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-col-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> From the 17th century, Akans emerged from what is believed to have been the Bonoman area, to create several Akan states, mainly based on gold trading.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> These states included Bonoman (Brong-Ahafo region), Ashanti (<a href="/wiki/Ashanti_Region" title="Ashanti Region">Ashanti Region</a>), <a href="/wiki/Denkyira" title="Denkyira">Denkyira</a> (<a href="/wiki/Central_Region_(Ghana)" title="Central Region (Ghana)">Western North region</a>), <a href="/wiki/Mankessim_Kingdom" title="Mankessim Kingdom">Mankessim Kingdom</a> (<a href="/wiki/Western_Region_(Ghana)" title="Western Region (Ghana)">Central region</a>), and <a href="/wiki/Akwamu" title="Akwamu">Akwamu</a> <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Region_(Ghana)" title="Eastern Region (Ghana)">(Eastern region)</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-col_25-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-col-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> By the 19th century, the territory of the southern part of Ghana was included in the <a href="/wiki/Ashanti_Empire" title="Ashanti Empire">Kingdom of Ashanti</a>, one of the influential states in sub-saharan Africa prior to the onset of colonialism.<sup id="cite_ref-col_25-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-col-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The government of the Ashanti Empire operated first as a loose network and eventually as a centralised kingdom with an advanced, highly specialised bureaucracy centred in the capital city of <a href="/wiki/Kumasi" title="Kumasi">Kumasi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-col_25-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-col-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Prior to Akan contact with Europeans, the Akan people created an advanced economy based on principally gold and <a href="/wiki/Gold_bar" title="Gold bar">gold bar</a> <a href="/wiki/Commodity" title="Commodity">commodities</a> then traded with the states of Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-col_25-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-col-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Ga-Dangme and Ewe migrated westward from south-western Nigeria because of pressure from incessant tribal wars.The Ewe migrated with their other Gbe speaking kinsmen and in transition,settled in Ketou in Benin Republic, Tado in Togo and Nortsie in Togo as their final dispersal point. Their dispersal from Nortsie was necessitated by the high handed rule of King Agorkorli (Agor Akorlie). The Ga- Dangme occupy the Greater Accra Region and parts of the Eastern Region, while the Ewe are found in the <a href="/wiki/Volta_Region" title="Volta Region">Volta Region</a> as well as the neighboring Togo, Benin and Nigeria ( around Badagry area).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="European_contact_and_colonialism">European contact and colonialism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ghana&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: European contact and colonialism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Slave_Coast_of_West_Africa" title="Slave Coast of West Africa">Slave Coast of West Africa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Slave_Coast" title="Dutch Slave Coast">Dutch Slave Coast</a></div><div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Elmina_slave_castle.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Elmina_slave_castle.jpg/220px-Elmina_slave_castle.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="167" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Elmina_slave_castle.jpg/330px-Elmina_slave_castle.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Elmina_slave_castle.jpg/440px-Elmina_slave_castle.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1152" data-file-height="872" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Elmina_slave_castle.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese</a> established the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Gold_Coast" title="Portuguese Gold Coast">Portuguese Gold Coast</a> with the construction of <a href="/wiki/Elmina_Castle" title="Elmina Castle">Elmina Castle</a> (<i>Castelo da Mina</i>) by <a href="/wiki/Diogo_de_Azambuja" title="Diogo de Azambuja">Diogo de Azambuja</a> in 1482, making it the oldest <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_colonial_architecture" title="Portuguese colonial architecture">European building</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sub-Saharan_Africa" title="Sub-Saharan Africa">sub-Saharan Africa</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Akan trade with European states began after contact with the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese</a> in the 15th century.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Early European contact by the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_people" title="Portuguese people">Portuguese people</a>, who came to the <a href="/wiki/Gold_Coast_(region)" title="Gold Coast (region)">Gold Coast region</a> in the 15th century to trade and then established the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Gold_Coast" title="Portuguese Gold Coast">Portuguese Gold Coast</a> (Costa do Ouro), focused on the extensive availability of gold.<sup id="cite_ref-History_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> The Portuguese built a trading lodge at a coastal settlement called Anomansah (the perpetual drink) which they renamed <a href="/wiki/Elmina" title="Elmina">São Jorge da Mina</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-History_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In 1481, King <a href="/wiki/John_II_of_Portugal" title="John II of Portugal">John II of Portugal</a> commissioned <a href="/wiki/Diogo_de_Azambuja" title="Diogo de Azambuja">Diogo de Azambuja</a> to build the <a href="/wiki/Elmina_Castle" title="Elmina Castle">Elmina Castle</a>, which was completed in three years.<sup id="cite_ref-History_35-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> By 1598, the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_people" title="Dutch people">Dutch</a> had joined the Portuguese in the gold trade, establishing the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Gold_Coast" title="Dutch Gold Coast">Dutch Gold Coast</a> (Nederlandse Bezittingen ter Kuste van Guinea) and building forts at <a href="/wiki/Fort_Komenda" title="Fort Komenda">Fort Komenda</a> and Kormantsi.<sup id="cite_ref-Ghana_book_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ghana_book-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> In 1617, the Dutch captured the Elmina Castle from the Portuguese and <a href="/wiki/Axim" title="Axim">Axim</a> in 1642 (<a href="/wiki/Fort_Saint_Anthony" title="Fort Saint Anthony">Fort St Anthony</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-Ghana_book_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ghana_book-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other European traders had joined in gold trading by the mid-17th century, most notably the <a href="/wiki/Swedes" title="Swedes">Swedes</a>, establishing the <a href="/wiki/Swedish_Gold_Coast" title="Swedish Gold Coast">Swedish Gold Coast</a> (Svenska Guldkusten), and <a href="/wiki/Denmark%E2%80%93Norway" title="Denmark–Norway">Denmark–Norway</a>, establishing the <a href="/wiki/Danish_Gold_Coast" title="Danish Gold Coast">Danish Gold Coast</a> (Danske Guldkyst <i>or</i> Dansk Guinea).<sup id="cite_ref-History_of_Ghana_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History_of_Ghana-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In addition to the gold trade, European traders participated in the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade" title="Atlantic slave trade">Atlantic slave trade</a> in this area.<sup id="cite_ref-Emmer_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Emmer-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> More than 30 forts and castles were built by the merchants. The Germans also established the <a href="/wiki/Brandenburger_Gold_Coast" title="Brandenburger Gold Coast">Brandenburger Gold Coast</a> <i>or</i> Groß Friedrichsburg).<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> In 1874, Great Britain established control over some parts of the country, assigning these areas the status of the <a href="/wiki/Gold_Coast_(British_colony)" title="Gold Coast (British colony)">British Gold Coast</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-colestablish_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-colestablish-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Many military engagements occurred between the British colonial powers and the various Akan nation-states. The Kingdom of Ashanti defeated the British a few times in the 100-year-long <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Ashanti_wars" title="Anglo-Ashanti wars">Anglo-Ashanti wars</a> but eventually lost with the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Golden_Stool" title="War of the Golden Stool">War of the Golden Stool</a> in 1900.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Chronology_of_world_history_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chronology_of_world_history-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:British_Ransack_Fomena_Palace.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/British_Ransack_Fomena_Palace.png/220px-British_Ransack_Fomena_Palace.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/British_Ransack_Fomena_Palace.png/330px-British_Ransack_Fomena_Palace.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/British_Ransack_Fomena_Palace.png/440px-British_Ransack_Fomena_Palace.png 2x" data-file-width="1694" data-file-height="1168" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:British_Ransack_Fomena_Palace.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>During the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Ashanti_Wars" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglo-Ashanti Wars">Anglo-Ashanti Wars</a>, British troops ransacking a <a href="/wiki/Fomena" title="Fomena">Fomena</a> chief's palace en route to Kumasi in 1874</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transition_to_independence">Transition to independence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ghana&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Transition to independence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Kwame_Nkrumah_(JFKWHP-AR6409-A).jpg" class="image"><img alt="Kwame Nkrumah" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Kwame_Nkrumah_%28JFKWHP-AR6409-A%29.jpg/170px-Kwame_Nkrumah_%28JFKWHP-AR6409-A%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Kwame_Nkrumah_%28JFKWHP-AR6409-A%29.jpg/255px-Kwame_Nkrumah_%28JFKWHP-AR6409-A%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/dc/Kwame_Nkrumah_%28JFKWHP-AR6409-A%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="320" data-file-height="480" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Kwame_Nkrumah_(JFKWHP-AR6409-A).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Kwame_Nkrumah" title="Kwame Nkrumah">Kwame Nkrumah</a>, first President of Ghana</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Dominion_of_Ghana" title="Dominion of Ghana">Dominion of Ghana</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ghana_Independence_Act_1957" title="Ghana Independence Act 1957">Ghana Independence Act 1957</a></div>

data-framerate="23.976023976024" /></video></div><div class="thumbcaption">Celebrations marking Ghana's independence on 6 March 1957</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>In 1947, the newly formed <a href="/wiki/United_Gold_Coast_Convention" title="United Gold Coast Convention">United Gold Coast Convention</a> led by <a href="/wiki/The_Big_Six_(Ghana)" title="The Big Six (Ghana)">"The Big Six"</a> called for "self-government within the shortest possible time" following the <a href="/wiki/1946_Gold_Coast_general_election" title="1946 Gold Coast general election">1946 Gold Coast legislative election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-History_of_Ghana_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History_of_Ghana-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Kwame_Nkrumah" title="Kwame Nkrumah">Kwame Nkrumah</a>, a Ghanaian nationalist who led Ghana from 1957 to 1966 as the country's first <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Ghana" title="Prime Minister of Ghana">prime minister</a> and <a href="/wiki/President_of_Ghana" title="President of Ghana">president</a>, formed the <a href="/wiki/Convention_People%27s_Party" title="Convention People&#39;s Party">Convention People's Party</a> in 1949 with the motto "self-government now".<sup id="cite_ref-History_of_Ghana_37-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History_of_Ghana-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The party initiated a "positive action" campaign involving non-violent protests, strikes and non-cooperation with the British authorities. Nkrumah was arrested and sentenced to one year imprisonment during this time. In the Gold Coast's <a href="/wiki/1951_Gold_Coast_general_election" title="1951 Gold Coast general election">1951 general election</a>, he was elected to Parliament and released from prison to become leader of government business.<sup id="cite_ref-History_of_Ghana_37-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History_of_Ghana-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> He became the prime minister in 1952. He improved the infrastructure of the country, and his Africanisation policies created better career opportunities for Ghanaians.
</p><p>On 6 March 1957 at midnight, the Gold Coast, Ashanti, the Northern Territories, and <a href="/wiki/British_Togoland" title="British Togoland">British Togoland</a> were unified as one single independent dominion within the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">British Commonwealth</a> under the name Ghana. This was done under the <a href="/wiki/Ghana_Independence_Act_1957" title="Ghana Independence Act 1957">Ghana Independence Act 1957</a>. The current <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Ghana" title="Flag of Ghana">flag of Ghana</a>, consisting of the colours red, gold, green, and a black star, dates back to this unification.<sup id="cite_ref-Ghana_flag_and_description_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ghana_flag_and_description-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> It was designed by <a href="/wiki/Theodosia_Okoh" title="Theodosia Okoh">Theodosia Okoh</a>; the red represents the blood that was shed towards independence; the gold represents the <a href="/wiki/Industrial_mineral" title="Industrial mineral">industrial minerals</a> wealth of Ghana; the green symbolises the rich grasslands of Ghana, and the black star is the symbol of the Ghanaian people and African emancipation.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 1 July 1960, following the <a href="/wiki/1960_Ghanaian_constitutional_referendum" title="1960 Ghanaian constitutional referendum">Ghanaian constitutional referendum</a> and <a href="/wiki/1960_Ghanaian_presidential_election" title="1960 Ghanaian presidential election">Ghanaian presidential election</a>, Nkrumah declared Ghana a republic and assumed the presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-Universal_Newsreel_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Universal_Newsreel-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-First_For_Sub-Saharan_Africa_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-First_For_Sub-Saharan_Africa-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Exploring_Africa_–_Decolinization_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Exploring_Africa_–_Decolinization-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-History_of_Ghana_37-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History_of_Ghana-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> 6 March is the nation's <a href="/wiki/Independence_Day_(Ghana)" title="Independence Day (Ghana)">Independence Day</a>, and 1 July is celebrated as <a href="/wiki/Republic_Day" title="Republic Day">Republic Day</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> At the time of independence Nkrumah declared, "My first objective is to abolish from Ghana poverty, ignorance, and disease. We shall measure our progress by the improvement in the health of our people; by the number of children in school, and by the quality of their education; by the availability of water and electricity in our towns and villages; and by the happiness which our people take in being able to manage their own affairs. The welfare of our people is our chief pride, and it is by this that my government will ask to be judged.".<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nkrumah led an <a href="/wiki/Authoritarianism" title="Authoritarianism">authoritarian</a> regime in Ghana, as he repressed political opposition and conducted elections that were not free and fair.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> In 1964, a <a href="/wiki/1964_Ghanaian_constitutional_referendum" title="1964 Ghanaian constitutional referendum">constitutional amendment</a> made Ghana a <a href="/wiki/One-party_state" title="One-party state">one-party state</a>, with Nkrumah as <a href="/wiki/President_for_life" title="President for life">president for life</a> of both the nation and its party.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nkrumah was the first African head of state to promote the concept of <a href="/wiki/Pan-Africanism" title="Pan-Africanism">Pan-Africanism</a>, which he had been introduced to during his studies at <a href="/wiki/Lincoln_University_(Pennsylvania)" title="Lincoln University (Pennsylvania)">Lincoln University</a>, Pennsylvania in the United States, at the time when <a href="/wiki/Marcus_Garvey" title="Marcus Garvey">Marcus Garvey</a> was popular for his "Back to Africa Movement".<sup id="cite_ref-History_of_Ghana_37-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History_of_Ghana-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Nkrumah merged the teachings of Garvey, <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr." title="Martin Luther King Jr.">Martin Luther King Jr.</a> and the naturalised Ghanaian scholar <a href="/wiki/W._E._B._Du_Bois" title="W. E. B. Du Bois">W. E. B. Du Bois</a> into the formation of 1960s Ghana.<sup id="cite_ref-History_of_Ghana_37-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-History_of_Ghana-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Osagyefo Dr. Kwame Nkrumah, as he became known, played an instrumental part in the founding of the <a href="/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement" title="Non-Aligned Movement">Non-Aligned Movement</a>, and in establishing the <a href="/wiki/Kwame_Nkrumah_Ideological_Institute" title="Kwame Nkrumah Ideological Institute">Kwame Nkrumah Ideological Institute</a> to teach his ideologies of <a href="/wiki/Communism" title="Communism">communism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Socialism" title="Socialism">socialism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> His life achievements were recognised by Ghanaians during his centenary birthday celebration, and the day was instituted as a <a href="/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Ghana" title="Public holidays in Ghana">public holiday in Ghana</a> (<a href="/wiki/Founders%27_Day_(Ghana)" title="Founders&#39; Day (Ghana)">Founders' Day</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Operation_Cold_Chop_and_aftermath">Operation Cold Chop and aftermath</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ghana&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Operation Cold Chop and aftermath">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Ghana_(1966%E2%80%931979)" title="History of Ghana (1966–1979)">History of Ghana (1966–1979)</a></div>
<p>The government of Nkrumah was subsequently overthrown by a coup by the <a href="/wiki/Ghana_Armed_Forces" title="Ghana Armed Forces">Ghana Armed Forces</a> codenamed "Operation Cold Chop". This occurred while Nkrumah was abroad with <a href="/wiki/Zhou_Enlai" title="Zhou Enlai">Zhou Enlai</a> in the People's Republic of China, on a fruitless mission to <a href="/wiki/Hanoi" title="Hanoi">Hanoi</a> in Vietnam to help end the <a href="/wiki/Vietnam_War" title="Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a>. The coup took place on 24 February 1966, led by Colonel <a href="/wiki/Emmanuel_Kwasi_Kotoka" title="Emmanuel Kwasi Kotoka">Emmanuel Kwasi Kotoka</a> and Brigadier Akwasi Afrifa. The <a href="/wiki/National_Liberation_Council" title="National Liberation Council">National Liberation Council</a> was formed, chaired by Lieutenant General <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Arthur_Ankrah" title="Joseph Arthur Ankrah">Joseph A. Ankrah</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-political_&amp;_social_thought_of_Kwame_Nkrumah_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-political_&amp;_social_thought_of_Kwame_Nkrumah-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A series of alternating military and civilian governments, often affected by economic instabilities,<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> ruled Ghana from 1966 to 1981, ending with the ascension to power of <a href="/wiki/Flight_lieutenant" title="Flight lieutenant">Flight Lieutenant</a> <a href="/wiki/Jerry_Rawlings" title="Jerry Rawlings">Jerry John Rawlings</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Provisional_National_Defence_Council" title="Provisional National Defence Council">Provisional National Defence Council</a> in 1981.<sup id="cite_ref-Flight_Lieutenant_Jerry_John_Rawlings_(J.J_Rawlings)_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Flight_Lieutenant_Jerry_John_Rawlings_(J.J_Rawlings)-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> These changes resulted in the suspension of the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Ghana" title="Constitution of Ghana">Constitution of Ghana</a> in 1981 and the banning of <a href="/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Ghana" title="List of political parties in Ghana">political parties</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rawlings:_The_legacy_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rawlings:_The_legacy-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> The economy soon declined, so Rawlings negotiated a structural adjustment plan, changing many old economic policies, and economic growth recovered during the mid-1980s.<sup id="cite_ref-Rawlings:_The_legacy_61-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rawlings:_The_legacy-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> A new constitution restoring <a href="/wiki/Multi-party_system" title="Multi-party system">multi-party system</a> politics was promulgated in the <a href="/wiki/1992_Ghanaian_presidential_election" title="1992 Ghanaian presidential election">presidential election of 1992</a>; Rawlings was elected as president of Ghana then, and again in the <a href="/wiki/1996_Ghanaian_general_election" title="1996 Ghanaian general election">general election of 1996</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Elections_in_Ghana_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Elections_in_Ghana-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At least 1,000 and as many as 2,000 people were killed during the <a href="/wiki/Konkomba%E2%80%93Nanumba_conflict" title="Konkomba–Nanumba conflict">conflict</a> between the <a href="/wiki/Konkomba_people" title="Konkomba people">Konkomba</a> and other ethnic groups such as the Nanumba, <a href="/wiki/Dagomba_people" title="Dagomba people">Dagomba</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gonja_people" title="Gonja people">Gonja</a>, while 150,000 people were displaced as part of the tribal war in <a href="/wiki/Northern_Region_(Ghana)" title="Northern Region (Ghana)">Northern Ghana</a> in 1994.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="21st_century">21st century</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ghana&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: 21st century">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Kpetoee.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Kpetoee.jpg/220px-Kpetoee.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Kpetoee.jpg/330px-Kpetoee.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Kpetoee.jpg/440px-Kpetoee.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="667" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Kpetoee.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Traditional <a href="/wiki/Chieftaincy" class="mw-redirect" title="Chieftaincy">chiefs</a> in Ghana in 2015</div></div></div>
<p>Winning the <a href="/wiki/2000_Ghanaian_general_election" title="2000 Ghanaian general election">2000 general election</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Kufuor" title="John Kufuor">John Kufuor</a> of the <a href="/wiki/New_Patriotic_Party" title="New Patriotic Party">New Patriotic Party</a> was sworn into office as president of Ghana on 7 January 2001 and attained the presidency again in the <a href="/wiki/2004_Ghanaian_general_election" title="2004 Ghanaian general election">2004 election</a>, thus also serving two terms (the term limit) as president of Ghana and thus marking the first time under the fourth republic that power was transferred from one legitimately elected head of state and head of government to another.<sup id="cite_ref-Elections_in_Ghana_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Elections_in_Ghana-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nana Akufo-Addo, the ruling party candidate, was defeated in a very close election by <a href="/wiki/John_Atta_Mills" title="John Atta Mills">John Atta Mills</a> of the <a href="/wiki/National_Democratic_Congress_(Ghana)" title="National Democratic Congress (Ghana)">National Democratic Congress</a> following the <a href="/wiki/2008_Ghanaian_general_election" title="2008 Ghanaian general election">2008 general election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Mills died of natural causes and was succeeded by Vice President <a href="/wiki/John_Mahama" title="John Mahama">John Mahama</a> on 24 July 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Following the <a href="/wiki/2012_Ghanaian_general_election" title="2012 Ghanaian general election">2012 general election</a>, Mahama became president, <sup id="cite_ref-Ghanaian_President_John_Dramani_Mahama_sworn_in_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ghanaian_President_John_Dramani_Mahama_sworn_in-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> and Ghana was a stable democracy.<sup id="cite_ref-Elections_in_Ghana_62-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Elections_in_Ghana-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> As a result of the <a href="/wiki/2016_Ghanaian_general_election" title="2016 Ghanaian general election">2016 general election</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Nana_Akufo-Addo" title="Nana Akufo-Addo">Nana Akufo-Addo</a> became president on 7 January 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> He was re-elected after a tightly contested <a href="/wiki/2020_Ghanaian_general_election" title="2020 Ghanaian general election">election in 2020</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 11 June 2021, Ghana inaugurated Green Ghana Day in an aim of planting 5 million trees in a concentrating effort to preserve the country's cover of rainforest to combat deforestation.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ghana&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Ghana" title="Geography of Ghana">Geography of Ghana</a> and <a href="/wiki/Geology_of_Ghana" title="Geology of Ghana">Geology of Ghana</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"/><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:322px;max-width:322px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:176px;max-width:176px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:203px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Ghana_Topography.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Ghana_Topography.png/174px-Ghana_Topography.png" decoding="async" width="174" height="204" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Ghana_Topography.png/261px-Ghana_Topography.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Ghana_Topography.png/348px-Ghana_Topography.png 2x" data-file-width="794" data-file-height="931" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption">Ghana-Togo <a href="/wiki/Topography" title="Topography">topography</a>−<a href="/wiki/Topographic_map" title="Topographic map">topographic map</a></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:142px;max-width:142px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:203px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Ghana_sat.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Ghana_sat.png/140px-Ghana_sat.png" decoding="async" width="140" height="203" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Ghana_sat.png/210px-Ghana_sat.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Ghana_sat.png/280px-Ghana_sat.png 2x" data-file-width="534" data-file-height="774" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption">Ghana <a href="/wiki/Satellite_imagery" title="Satellite imagery">satellite image</a></div></div></div></div></div>
<p>Ghana is located on the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Guinea" title="Gulf of Guinea">Gulf of Guinea</a>, only a few degrees north of the <a href="/wiki/Equator" title="Equator">Equator</a>, therefore giving it a warm climate.<sup id="cite_ref-Geography_Physical_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geography_Physical-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Ghana spans an area of 238,535&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (92,099&#160;sq&#160;mi) and has an Atlantic coastline that stretches 560 kilometres (350 miles) on the Gulf of Guinea in the Atlantic Ocean to its south.<sup id="cite_ref-Geography_Physical_72-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geography_Physical-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Dodi_Island" title="Dodi Island">Dodi Island</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bobowasi_Island" title="Bobowasi Island">Bobowasi Island</a> are near the south coast.<sup id="cite_ref-Ghana_low_plains_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ghana_low_plains-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> It lies between latitudes 4°45'N and 11°N, and longitudes 1°15'E and 3°15'W. The <a href="/wiki/Prime_meridian" title="Prime meridian">prime meridian</a> passes through Ghana, specifically through <a href="/wiki/Tema" title="Tema">Tema</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Geography_Physical_72-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geography_Physical-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Ghana is geographically closer to the "centre" of the Earth than any other country, since the notional centre, (0°, 0°) is located in the Atlantic Ocean approximately 614&#160;km (382&#160;mi) off the south-east coast of Ghana.
</p><p>Grasslands mixed with south coastal shrublands and forests dominate Ghana, with forest extending northward from the coast 320 kilometres (200 miles) and eastward for a maximum of about 270 kilometres (170 miles) with several locations for mining of industrial minerals and timber.<sup id="cite_ref-Geography_Physical_72-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geography_Physical-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Ghana is home to five terrestrial ecoregions: <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Guinean_forests" title="Eastern Guinean forests">Eastern Guinean forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guinean_forest%E2%80%93savanna_mosaic" title="Guinean forest–savanna mosaic">Guinean forest–savanna mosaic</a>, <a href="/wiki/West_Sudanian_savanna" title="West Sudanian savanna">West Sudanian savanna</a>, <a href="/wiki/Central_African_mangroves" title="Central African mangroves">Central African mangroves</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Guinean_mangroves" title="Guinean mangroves">Guinean mangroves</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> It had a 2018 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 4.53/10, ranking it 112th globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
The <a href="/wiki/White_Volta" title="White Volta">White Volta</a> River and its tributary <a href="/wiki/Black_Volta" title="Black Volta">Black Volta</a>, flow south through Ghana to <a href="/wiki/Lake_Volta" title="Lake Volta">Lake Volta</a>, the world's <a href="/wiki/List_of_reservoirs_by_volume" title="List of reservoirs by volume">third-largest reservoir by volume</a> and largest by surface area, formed by the hydroelectric <a href="/wiki/Akosombo_Dam" title="Akosombo Dam">Akosombo Dam</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> completed in 1965. The Volta flows out of Lake Volta into the Gulf of Guinea.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> The northernmost part of Ghana is Pulmakong and the southernmost part of Ghana is <a href="/wiki/Cape_Three_Points" title="Cape Three Points">Cape Three Points</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Geography_Physical_72-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geography_Physical-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></p><div style="clear:both;"></div>

<td><a href="/wiki/Tamale,_Ghana" title="Tamale, Ghana">Tamale</a>
</td>
<td style="text-align:center;">Largest settlement in the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dagbon" title="Kingdom of Dagbon">Kingdom of Dagbon</a> and gateway to <a href="/wiki/Mole_National_Park" title="Mole National Park">Mole National Park</a>.
</td></tr>
